# Washington (state)

"Washington state" redirects here. Not to be confused with [Wa State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wa_State). For the proposed statehood of Washington, D.C., see [District of Columbia statehood movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_statehood_movement). **Washington** is a [state](18618239.md) in the [Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Northwest) region of the [United States](3434750.md). It is often referred to as **Washington state**[^cite_note-5] to distinguish it from the [national capital](108956.md);[^cite_note-6] both are named after [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington), a [U.S. Founding Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Founding_Father) and the first [U.S. president](24113.md). Washington borders the [Pacific Ocean](23070.md) to the west, [Oregon](26811621.md) to the south, and [Idaho](14607.md) to the east; it shares [an international border](630260.md) with the [Canadian](5042916.md) province of [British Columbia](3392.md) to the north. [Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia,_Washington) is the [state capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_capitals_in_the_United_States), and the state's most populous city is [Seattle](11388236.md).

Washington is the [18th-largest state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area), with an area of 71,362 sq mi (184,830 km<sup>2</sup>), and the [13th-most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population) state, with a population of over 8 million.[^cite_note-Quick_Facts-2] The majority of Washington's residents live in the [Seattle metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_metropolitan_area), the center of transportation, business, and industry on [Puget Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puget_Sound),[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] an inlet of the Pacific Ocean consisting of numerous islands, deep [fjords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fjord) and bays carved out by glaciers. The remainder of the state consists of deep [temperate rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_rainforest) in the west; [mountain ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_range) in the west, center, northeast, and far southeast, and a semi-arid basin region in the east, center, and south, given over to intensive agriculture. Washington is the second-most populous state on the [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States) and in the Western United States, after [California](5407.md). [Mount Rainier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Rainier), an active [stratovolcano](162404.md), is the state's highest elevation at 14,400 feet (4,389 meters),[^cite_note-9] and is the most [topographically prominent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topographically_prominent) mountain in the [contiguous U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contiguous_U.S.)

Washington is a leading [lumber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumber) producer, the largest producer of apples, hops, pears, blueberries, spearmint oil, and sweet cherries in the U.S., and ranks high in the production of apricots, asparagus, dry edible peas, grapes, lentils, peppermint oil, potatoes and wheat.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-agr.wa.gov-11] Livestock, livestock products, and commercial fishing—particularly of [salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon), [halibut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halibut), and [bottomfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bottomfish)—are also significant contributors to the state's economy.[^cite_note-Smith-12] Washington ranks third in [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine) production. Manufacturing industries in Washington include aircraft, missiles, shipbuilding, and other transportation equipment, food processing, metals, and metal products, chemicals, and machinery.[^cite_note-13]

The state was formed from the western part of the [Washington Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Territory), the claims to which were ceded by the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) in the [Oregon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Treaty) of 1846. Most of the land that would become Washington state was ceded by the Indigenous peoples of the region in the [Stevens treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevens_Treaties_\(disambiguation\)) of 1854–1855. It was admitted to the Union as the 42nd state in 1889. One of the [wealthiest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_income) and most [socially liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socially_liberal) states in the country,[^cite_note-14] Washington consistently ranks among the top states for highest [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) and employment rates.[^cite_note-15] It was one of the first states (alongside [Colorado](5399.md)) to [legalize medicinal and recreational cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Washington_\(state\)),[^cite_note-16] was among the first states to introduce [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Washington_\(state\)),[^cite_note-17] and permitted [legal abortions on request](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States#History) before *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* in 1973.[^cite_note-Connelly-18]

## Etymology

Washington was named after [President](24113.md) [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) by an act of the [United States Congress](31756.md) during the creation of [Washington Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Territory) in 1853; the territory was originally to be named "Columbia", for the [Columbia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_River) and the [Columbia District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_District), but Kentucky representative [Richard H. Stanton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_H._Stanton) found the name too similar to the [District of Columbia](108956.md) (the nation's capital, itself containing the city of Washington), and proposed naming the new territory after President Washington.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Longview_History-20][^cite_note-Cowlitz_Timeline-21] Thus, Washington is the only U.S. state named after a president.[^cite_note-22]

Confusion between the state of Washington and the city of Washington, D.C., led to renaming proposals during the statehood process for Washington in 1889, including [David Dudley Field II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Dudley_Field_II)'s suggestion to name the new state "Tacoma"; these proposals failed to garner support.[^cite_note-23] Washington, D.C.'s, [own statehood movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_statehood_movement) in the 21st century has included a proposal to use the name "State of Washington, Douglass Commonwealth", which would conflict with the current state of Washington.[^cite_note-Bush-24] Residents of Washington (known as "Washingtonians") and the [Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Northwest) simply refer to the state as "Washington", and the nation's capital "Washington, D.C.", "the other Washington",[^cite_note-25] or simply "D.C."

## History

Main article: [History of Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Washington_\(state\))

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Washington (state) history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Washington_\(state\)_history).

### Early history



A farm and barren hills near [Riverside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverside,_Washington), in north-central Washington

The skeletal remains of [Kennewick Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kennewick_Man), one of the oldest and most complete human remains found in North America, were discovered in Washington in 1996 and radiocarbon dated to between 8,400 and 8,690 years [Before Present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_Present).[^cite_note-26] The region has been home to many established tribes of indigenous peoples for thousands of years. They are notable for their ornately carved welcome figures, canoes, long houses and masks. Prominent among their industries were [salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon) fishing and, notably among the [Makah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makah), whale hunting.[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28] The peoples of the Interior had a different subsistence-based culture based on hunting, food-gathering and some forms of agriculture, as well as a dependency on salmon from the Columbia and its tributaries.

The area has been known to host [megathrust earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megathrust_earthquake) in the past, the last being the [Cascadia earthquake of 1700](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1700_Cascadia_earthquake).[^cite_note-29]

### European exploration

Main articles: [Oregon Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Country) and [Columbia District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_District)

The first recorded European landing on the Washington coast was by Spanish Captain Don [Bruno de Heceta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_de_Heceta) in 1775 at what is now [Point Grenville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Point_Grenville),[^cite_note-hl-30][^cite_note-31] on board the *Santiago*, part of a two-ship [flotilla](981096.md) with the *Sonora*. He claimed the coastal lands up to [Prince William Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_William_Sound) for Spain as part of their claimed rights under the [Treaty of Tordesillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas), which they maintained made the Pacific a "Spanish lake" and all its shores part of the Spanish Empire. Soon thereafter, the [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox) epidemic of the 1770s devastated the Native American population.[^cite_note-32]

In 1778, [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) explorer Captain [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) sighted [Cape Flattery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Flattery), at the entrance to the [Strait of Juan de Fuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Juan_de_Fuca), but Cook did not realize the strait existed.[^cite_note-\:0-33] It was not discovered until [Charles William Barkley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_William_Barkley), captain of the *[Imperial Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Eagle_\(ship\))*, sighted it in 1787.[^cite_note-\:0-33] The straits were further explored by Spanish explorers [Manuel Quimper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Quimper) in 1790 and [Francisco de Eliza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_de_Eliza) in 1791,[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35] and British explorer [George Vancouver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Vancouver) in 1792.[^cite_note-36]

### European settlement

Main article: [Oregon pioneer history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_pioneer_history)

The [Nootka Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nootka_Convention) of 1790 ended Spanish claims of exclusivity and opened the Northwest Coast to explorers and traders from other nations, most notably Britain, Russia, and the fledgling United States.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-38] American captain [Robert Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gray_\(sea_captain\)) (for whom [Grays Harbor County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grays_Harbor_County,_Washington) is named) then discovered the mouth of the Columbia River. He named the river after his ship, the [*Columbia*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Rediviva).[^cite_note-39] Beginning in 1792, Gray established trade in [sea otter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_otter) pelts. The [Lewis and Clark Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_and_Clark_Expedition) entered the state on October 10, 1805.[^cite_note-40]

Explorer [David Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Thompson_\(explorer\)), on his voyage down the Columbia River, camped at the confluence with the Snake River on July 9, 1811,[^cite_note-41] and erected a pole and a notice claiming the territory for Great Britain and stating the intention of the [North West Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_Company) to build a trading post at the site.



Fur trading at [Fort Nez Percés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Nez_Perc%C3%A9s) in 1841

Britain and the United States agreed to what has since been described as "joint occupancy" of lands west of the [Continental Divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Divide_of_the_Americas) to the Pacific Ocean as part of the [Anglo-American Convention of 1818](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-American_Convention_of_1818), which established the [49th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north) as the international boundary west from [Lake of the Woods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_of_the_Woods) to the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains).[^cite_note-42] Resolution of the territorial and treaty issues west to the Pacific was deferred until a later time. In 1819, Spain ceded its rights north of the [42nd parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/42nd_parallel_north) to the United States.[^cite_note-43]

Negotiations with Great Britain over the next few decades failed to settle upon a compromise boundary and the [Oregon boundary dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_boundary_dispute) was highly contested between Britain and the United States. Disputed joint occupancy by Britain and the U.S. lasted for several decades. With American settlers pouring into [Oregon Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Country), [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson's_Bay_Company), which had previously discouraged settlement because it conflicted with the fur trade, reversed its position in an attempt to maintain British control of the [Columbia District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_District).[^cite_note-44]

Fur trapper [James Sinclair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Sinclair_\(fur_trader\)), on orders from [Sir George Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Simpson_\(businessman\)), Governor of the Hudson's Bay Company, led some 200 settlers from the [Red River Colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_River_Colony) west in 1841 to settle on Hudson Bay Company farms near [Fort Vancouver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Vancouver).[^cite_note-45] The party crossed the Rockies into the [Columbia Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Valley), near present-day [Radium Hot Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radium_Hot_Springs_\(British_Columbia\)), British Columbia, then traveled south-west down the [Kootenai River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kootenai_River) and Columbia River. Despite such efforts, Britain eventually ceded all claims to land south of the 49th parallel to the United States in the [Oregon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Treaty) on June 15, 1846.[^cite_note-OregonTreaty-46]

In 1836, a group of missionaries, including [Marcus Whitman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Whitman), established several missions and Whitman's own settlement Waiilatpu, in what is now southeastern Washington state, near present-day [Walla Walla County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walla_Walla_County), in the territory of both the [Cayuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cayuse_people) and the [Nez Perce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nez_Perce) Indian tribes.[^cite_note-47] Whitman's settlement would in 1843 help the [Oregon Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Trail), the overland emigration route to the west, get established for thousands of emigrants in the following decades. Whitman provided medical care for the Native Americans, but when Indian patients—lacking immunity to new, "European" diseases—died in striking numbers, while at the same time many white patients recovered, they held "medicine man" Marcus Whitman personally responsible, and executed Whitman and twelve other white settlers. This was called the [Whitman massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitman_massacre) in 1847.[^cite_note-48] This event triggered the [Cayuse War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cayuse_War) between settlers and Indians.

[Fort Nisqually](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Nisqually), a farm and trading post of the Hudson's Bay Company and the first European settlement in the [Puget Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puget_Sound) area, was founded in 1833.[^cite_note-49] Black pioneer [George Washington Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_Bush) and his Caucasian wife, Isabella James Bush, from [Missouri](19571.md) and [Tennessee](30395.md), respectively, led four white families into the territory and founded New Market, now [Tumwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumwater), in 1846.[^cite_note-50] They settled in Washington to avoid [Oregon](26811621.md)'s [black exclusion law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_black_exclusion_laws), which prohibited [African Americans](2154.md) from entering the territory while simultaneously prohibiting [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] After them, many more settlers, migrating overland along the Oregon Trail, wandered north to settle in the Puget Sound area.

Spanish and Russian claims to the region were ceded in the early 19th century through a series of treaties. The Spanish signed the [Adams–Onís Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty) of 1819, and the Russians the [Russo-American Treaty of 1824](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-American_Treaty_of_1824) and [1825](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Saint_Petersburg_\(1825\)).

The [Oregon Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Question) remained contested between the United Kingdom and the United States until the 1846 [Oregon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Treaty) established the border between [British North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America) and the United States along the 49th parallel until the [Strait of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Georgia).[^cite_note-OregonTreaty-46] Vague wording in the treaty left the ownership of the [San Juan Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Islands) in doubt; during the so-called [Pig War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pig_War_\(1859\)), both nations agreed to a joint military occupation of the islands.[^cite_note-NPSPigWar-53] Kaiser [Wilhelm I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_I) of the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) was selected as an arbitrator to end the dispute, with a three-man commission ruling in favor of the United States in 1872. The border established by the Oregon Treaty and finalized by the arbitration in 1872 remains the boundary between Washington and [British Columbia](3392.md).

### Statehood

Main articles: [Oregon boundary dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_boundary_dispute), [Provisional Government of Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Oregon), [Oregon Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Treaty), [Oregon Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Territory), [Organic act § List of organic acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_act#List_of_organic_acts), [Washington Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Territory), [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union), and [Mount Baker gold rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker_gold_rush)



Yesler Way in Seattle, 1887

The growing population of Oregon Territory north of the Columbia River formally requested a new territory. As a result of the [Monticello Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monticello_Convention), held in present-day [Cowlitz County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowlitz_County), the [U.S. Congress](31756.md) passed legislation to create [Washington Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Territory). It was signed into law by [President](24113.md) [Millard Fillmore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millard_Fillmore) on March 2, 1853.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-Cowlitz_Timeline-21] The boundary of Washington Territory initially extended farther east than the present state, including what is now the [Idaho](14607.md) [panhandle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idaho_panhandle) and parts of western Montana, and picked up more land to the southeast that was left behind when Oregon was admitted as a state; the creation of [Idaho Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idaho_Territory) in 1863 established the final eastern border.[^cite_note-auto1-55]

A [Washington state constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Washington) was drafted and ratified in 1878, but it was never officially adopted.[^cite_note-auto1-55] Although never approved by the United States Congress, the 1878 constitution is an important historical document that shows the political thinking of the time; it was used extensively during the drafting of Washington state's 1889 constitution, the one and only official Constitution of the State of Washington. Washington became the [42nd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union) state of the United States on November 11, 1889.[^cite_note-56]

Early prominent industries in the new state included agriculture and lumber. In Eastern Washington, the [Yakima River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakima_River) Valley became known for its apple orchards,[^cite_note-57] while the growth of wheat using [dry farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_farming) techniques became particularly productive. Heavy rainfall to the west of the Cascade Range produced dense forests, and the ports along Puget Sound prospered from the manufacturing and shipping of lumber products, particularly the [Douglas fir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_fir). Other industries that developed in the state included fishing, salmon canning and mining.[^cite_note-Smith-12][^cite_note-58]

### Post-statehood



[Boeing B-17E Flying Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_B-17_Flying_Fortress) bombers under construction, circa 1942



Early eruption of Mt. St. Helens

For a long period, [Tacoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma,_Washington) had large smelters where gold, silver, copper, and lead ores were treated.[^cite_note-59] [Seattle](11388236.md) was the primary port for trade with Alaska and the rest of the country, and for a time, it possessed a large shipbuilding industry. The region around eastern Puget Sound developed heavy industry during the period including [World War I](4764461.md) and [World War II](32927.md), and the [Boeing](18933266.md) company became an established icon in the area.[^cite_note-60]

During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), a series of [hydroelectric dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_dams) were constructed along the Columbia River as part of a project to increase the production of electricity. This culminated in 1941 with the completion of the [Grand Coulee Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Coulee_Dam), the largest concrete structure in the United States and the largest dam in the world at its construction.[^cite_note-61]

During World War II, the state became a focus for war industries. While the Boeing Company produced many [heavy bombers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_bomber), ports in [Seattle](11388236.md), [Bremerton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bremerton), [Vancouver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver,_Washington), and [Tacoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma,_Washington) were available for the manufacture of warships. Seattle was the point of departure for many soldiers in the Pacific, several of whom were quartered at Fort Lawton, which later became [Discovery Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Park_\(Seattle\)).[^cite_note-62] In Eastern Washington, the [Hanford Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanford_Site) [atomic energy](22153.md) plant was opened in 1943 and played a major role in the construction of [atomic bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombs).[^cite_note-63]

After the end of World War II, and with the beginning of the [civil rights movement](49001.md), the state's growing [Black or African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_or_African-American) population's wages were 53% above the national average. The early diversification of Washington through the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) led to successful efforts at reducing discrimination in the workplace.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] In 1950, Seattle's first black representative for the [state's legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Legislature) was elected. At the [1970 U.S. census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_U.S._census), the black population grew to 7.13% of the total population.[^cite_note-66]

In 1970, the state was one of only four U.S. states to have been providing legal abortions before the 1973 [Supreme Court](31737.md) decision in *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* which loosened abortion laws nationwide.[^cite_note-Connelly-18][^cite_note-67]

On May 18, 1980, following a period of heavy tremors and small eruptions, the north face of [Mount St. Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_St._Helens) [slid off in the largest landslide in recorded history before erupting violently](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_eruption_of_Mount_St._Helens), destroying a large part of the top of the volcano. The eruption flattened the forest up to 12 mi (20 km) north of the volcano, killed 57 people, flooded the Columbia River and its tributaries with ash and mud, and blanketed large parts of Washington eastward and other surrounding states in ash, making day look like night.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

## Geography

See also: [Geography of Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Washington_\(state\)) and [Geology of the Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_the_Pacific_Northwest)

| --- |



The [Pacific coast](751843.md) of [Westport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westport,_Washington)

Washington is the northwesternmost state of the [contiguous United States](83759.md). It borders [Idaho](14607.md) to the east, bounded mostly by the meridian running north from the confluence of the [Snake River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snake_River) and [Clearwater River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clearwater_River_\(Idaho\)) (about 117°02'23" west), except for the southernmost section where the border follows the Snake River. Oregon is to the south, with the Columbia River forming the western part and the 46th parallel forming the eastern part of the Oregon–Washington border. During Washington's partition from Oregon, the original plan for the border followed the Columbia River east until the confluence with the Snake, and then would have followed the Snake River east. This was changed to keep [Walla Walla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walla_Walla_County,_Washington)'s fertile farmland in Washington.

To the west of Washington lies the Pacific Ocean.[^cite_note-Constitution-70] Its northern border lies mostly along the [49th parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49th_parallel_north), and then via marine boundaries through the [Strait of Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Georgia), [Haro Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haro_Strait), and [Strait of Juan de Fuca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Juan_de_Fuca), with the [Canadian province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_province) of [British Columbia](3392.md) to the north.[^cite_note-71]

Washington is part of a region known as the [Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Northwest), a term which always refers to at least Washington and Oregon, and may or may not include some or all the following, depending on the user's intent: Idaho, western [Montana](19978.md), [northern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_California), British Columbia, and [Alaska](624.md).

The high mountains of the [Cascade Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Range) run north–south, bisecting the state. In addition to [Western Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Washington) and [Eastern Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Washington), residents call the two parts of the state the "Westside" and the "Eastside", "Wet side" and "Dry side", or "Timberland" and "Wheatland", the latter pair more commonly in the names of region-specific businesses and institutions. These terms reflect the geography, climate, and industry of the land on both sides of the Cascades.

### Western Washington

| Washington (state) is located in Washington (state)Mount Baker[Mount Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker)Glacier Peak[Glacier Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier_Peak)Mount Rainier[Mount Rainier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Rainier)Mount St. Helens[Mount St. Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_St._Helens)Mount Adams[Mount Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Adams_\(Washington\)) A physical map of Washington with the volcanic peaks Mount Baker, Glacier Peak, Mount Rainier, Mount Adams, and Mount St Helens pinned. |
| --- |



[Mount Baker–Snoqualmie National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker%E2%80%93Snoqualmie_National_Forest)

From the [Cascade Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Mountains) westward, [Western Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Washington) has a mostly [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate), with mild temperatures and wet winters, autumns and springs, and relatively dry summers. The Cascade Range has several [volcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Volcanoes), which reach altitudes significantly higher than the rest of the mountains. From north to south, these major volcanoes are [Mount Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker), [Glacier Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier_Peak), [Mount Rainier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Rainier), [Mount St. Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_St._Helens), and [Mount Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Adams_\(Washington\)). All are active volcanoes.[^cite_note-72]

Mount Rainier—the tallest mountain in the state[^cite_note-usgs-73]—is 50 miles (80 km) south of the city of Seattle, from which it is prominently visible. The [United States Geological Survey](23814944.md) considers 14,400-foot-tall (4,389 m) Mount Rainier the most dangerous volcano in the Cascade Range, due to its proximity to the [Seattle metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_metropolitan_area), and most dangerous in the continental U.S. according to the [Decade Volcanoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decade_Volcanoes) list.[^cite_note-RainierHazard-74] It is also covered with more [glacial ice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_ice) than any other peak in the contiguous 48 states.[^cite_note-75]

Western Washington also is home of the [Olympic Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Mountains), far west on the [Olympic Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Peninsula), which support dense forests of conifers and areas of [temperate rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_rainforest). These deep forests, such as the [Hoh Rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoh_Rainforest), are among the only rainforests in the continental United States.[^cite_note-76] While Western Washington does not always experience a high amount of rainfall as measured in total inches of rain per year, it does consistently have more rainy days per year than most other places in the country.[^cite_note-77]

### Eastern Washington



Southeastern Washington

[Eastern Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Washington)—the part of the state east of the Cascades—has a relatively dry climate, in distinct contrast to the west side. It includes large areas of semiarid [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe) and a few truly arid [deserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert) in the [rain shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow) of the Cascades; the Hanford reservation receives an average annual precipitation of 6 to 7 inches (150 to 180 mm). Despite the limited amount of rainfall, [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) is an extremely important business throughout much of Eastern Washington, as the soil is highly productive and [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation), aided by dams along the Columbia River, is fairly widespread.[^cite_note-78] The spread of population in Eastern Washington is dominated by access to water, especially rivers. The main cities are all located alongside rivers or lakes; most of them are named after the river or lake they adjoin.

Farther east, the climate becomes less arid, with annual rainfall increasing as one goes east to 21.2 inches (540 mm) in Pullman, near the Washington–Idaho border.[^cite_note-79] The [Okanogan Highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okanogan_Highlands) and the rugged [Kettle River Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kettle_River_Range) and [Selkirk Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selkirk_Mountains) cover much of the state's northeastern quadrant. The [Palouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palouse) southeast region of Washington was grassland that has been mostly converted into farmland, and extends to the [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mountains_\(Pacific_Northwest\)).[^cite_note-80]

### Climate

See also: [Climate change in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Washington)



[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Washington, using 1991–2020 [climate normals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_normals).



[Dryland farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryland_farming) caused a large [dust storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dust_storm) in arid parts of Eastern Washington on October 4, 2009. Courtesy: NASA/GSFC, MODIS Rapid Response.

[^cite_note-nasa-81]

The state of Washington has a temperate climate. The eastern half of Washington has a [semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid) to [warm-summer mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warm-summer_mediterranean_climate). The western side of Washington and the coastal areas of the state have a cool [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) or warm-summer mediterranean climate. Major factors determining Washington's climate include the large semi-permanent [low pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_pressure) and [high pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-pressure_area) systems of the north Pacific Ocean, the continental air masses of North America, and the Olympic and Cascade mountains. In the spring and summer, a high-pressure [anticyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticyclone) system dominates the north Pacific Ocean, causing air to spiral out in a clockwise fashion. For Washington, this means [prevailing winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevailing_winds) from the northwest bring relatively cool air and a predictably [dry season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_season).[^cite_note-82]

In the autumn and winter, a low-pressure [cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclone) system, the [Aleutian Low](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleutian_Low), takes over in the north Pacific Ocean. The air spiraling inward in a counter-clockwise fashion causes Washington's prevailing winds to come from the southwest, and bring cool and overcast weather and a predictably [wet season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_season). The term "[Pineapple Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple_Express)" is used colloquially to describe [atmospheric river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_river) events, where repeated storm systems are directed by this persistent cyclone from the tropical Pacific regions a great distance into the Pacific Northwest. Western Washington is very cloudy during much of fall, winter, and early spring. [Seattle](11388236.md) averages the fewest sunshine hours of any major city in the United States.[^cite_note-83]

Despite Western Washington's marine climate similar to many coastal cities of Europe, there are exceptions such as the "Big Snow" events of 1880, 1881, 1893, and 1916,[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-Dorpat-85] and the "deep freeze" winters of 1883–1884, 1915–1916, 1949–1950, and 1955–1956, among others.[^cite_note-86] During these events, Western Washington had up to 6 feet (1.8 m) of snow, sub-zero (−18 °C) temperatures, three months with snow on the ground, and lakes and rivers frozen over for weeks.[^cite_note-Dorpat-85] Seattle's lowest officially recorded temperature is 0 °F (−18 °C) set on January 31, 1950, but low-altitude areas approximately three hours away from Seattle have recorded lows as cold as −48 °F (−44 °C).[^cite_note-87]

The [Southern Oscillation](317311.md) greatly influences weather during the cold season. During the El Niño phase, the jet stream enters the U.S. farther south through California, therefore late fall and winter are drier than normal with less snowpack. The La Niña phase reinforces the jet stream through the Pacific Northwest, causing Washington to have more rain and snow than average.[^cite_note-88]

In 2006, the Climate Impacts Group at the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) published *The Impacts of Climate Change in Washington's Economy*, a preliminary assessment of the risks and opportunities presented given the possibility of a rise in global temperatures and their effects on Washington state.[^cite_note-89]

#### Rain shadow effects

Main article: [Rain shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow)



Washington has extensive variation in rainfall.

Rainfall in Washington varies dramatically going from east to west. The Olympic Peninsula's western side receives as much as 160 inches (4,100 mm) of precipitation annually, making it the wettest area of the 48 conterminous states and a [temperate rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_rainforest). Weeks may pass without a clear day. The western slopes of the Cascade Range receive some of the heaviest annual snowfall in the country, in some places more than 200 inches or 5,100 millimeters water equivalent. In the rain shadow area east of the Cascades, the annual precipitation is only 6 inches (150 mm). Precipitation then increases again eastward toward the Rocky Mountains, about 120 miles (190 km) east of the Idaho border.

The Olympic mountains and Cascades compound this climatic pattern by causing [orographic lift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orographic_lift) of the air masses blown inland from the Pacific Ocean, resulting in the windward side of the mountains receiving high levels of precipitation and the leeward side receiving low levels. This occurs most dramatically around the Olympic Mountains and the Cascade Range. In both cases, the windward slopes facing southwest receive high precipitation and mild, cool temperatures.[^cite_note-auto-90]

The Puget Sound lowlands are known for clouds and rain in the winter. The western slopes of the Cascades receive larger amounts of precipitation, often falling as snow at higher elevations.[^cite_note-auto-90] [Mount Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker), near the state's northern border, is one of the snowiest places in the world. In 1999, it set the world record for snowfall in a single season—1,140 inches (95 ft; 29 m).[^cite_note-91]

East of the Cascades, a large region experiences strong rain shadow effects. Semi-arid conditions occur in much of Eastern Washington with the strongest rain shadow effects at the relatively low elevations of the central [Columbia Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Plateau)—especially the region just east of the Columbia River from about the Snake River to the [Okanagan Highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okanagan_Highland). Thus, instead of rain forests, much of Eastern Washington is covered with dry [grassland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland), [shrub-steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrub-steppe), and [dunes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dune).

#### Temperatures

The average annual temperature ranges from 51 °F (11 °C) on the Pacific coast, to 40 °F (4 °C) in the northeast. The lowest temperature recorded in the state was −48 °F (−44 °C) in [Winthrop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winthrop,_Washington) and [Mazama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazama,_Washington). The highest recorded temperature in the state was 120 °F (49 °C) at [Hanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanford,_Washington) on June 29, 2021.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] Both records were set east of the Cascades.

Western Washington is known for its mild climate, considerable fog, frequent cloud cover, long-lasting drizzles in the winter and warm, temperate summers. The eastern region, which does not benefit from the general moderating effect of the Pacific Ocean, occasionally experiences extreme climate. Arctic cold fronts in the winter and heat waves in the summer are not uncommon. In the Western region, temperatures have reached as high as 118 °F (48 °C) in [Maple Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_Valley,_Washington)[^cite_note-94] during the [June 2021 heat wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Western_North_America_heat_wave), and as low as −6 °F (−21 °C) in [Longview](137888.md),[^cite_note-95] and even −8 °F (−22 °C) in [Sammamish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammamish).[^cite_note-96]

### Flora and fauna

See also: [List of flora of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_flora_of_Washington), [List of fauna of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fauna_of_Washington), and [List of federal lands in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_lands_in_Washington)



Washington's national forests



[Black-tailed deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-tailed_deer) graze at Deer Park in [Olympic National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_National_Park)

Forests cover about half the state's land area, mostly west of the northern Cascades. Approximately two-thirds of Washington's forested area is publicly owned, including 64 percent of federal land.[^cite_note-107] Common trees and plants in the region are [camas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camassia), Douglas fir, hemlock, [penstemon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penstemon), ponderosa pine, [western red cedar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_red_cedar), and many species of ferns.[^cite_note-108] The state's areas of wilderness offer sanctuary, with substantially large populations of shorebirds and marine mammals. The Pacific shore surrounding the [San Juan Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Islands) is heavily inhabited by [killer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orca), gray, and humpback whales.[^cite_note-Clark-109]

In Eastern Washington, the flora is vastly different. [Tumbleweeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumbleweed) and [sagebrush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagebrush) dominate the landscape throughout large parts of the countryside. [Russian olives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaeagnus_angustifolia) and other trees are common alongside riverbanks. Apart from the riversides, large swaths of Eastern Washington have no naturally existing trees at all, though many trees have been planted and are irrigated by people. A wider variety of flora can be found in both the [Blue Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mountains_\(Oregon\)) and the eastern sides of the Cascades.

Mammals native to the state include the [bat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bat), [black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_black_bear), [bobcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobcat), [cougar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cougar), [coyote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyote), [deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deer), [elk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elk), [gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolf), [hare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hare), [moose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moose), [mountain beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_beaver), [muskrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muskrat), [opossum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opossum), [pocket gopher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pocket_gopher), [rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbit), [raccoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raccoon), [river otter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_river_otter), [skunk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skunk), and [tree squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_squirrel).[^cite_note-110] Because of the wide range of geography, the state of Washington is home to several different ecoregions, which allow for a varied range of bird species. This range includes raptors, shorebirds, woodland birds, grassland birds, ducks, and others.[^cite_note-111] There have also been a large number of species introduced to Washington, dating back to the early 18th century, including horses and burros.[^cite_note-112]

The [channel catfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_catfish), [lamprey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamprey), and [sturgeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sturgeon) are among the 400 known [freshwater fishes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_freshwater_fishes_of_Washington).[^cite_note-species-113][^cite_note-114] Along with the Cascades frog, there are several forms of snakes that define the most prominent [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_reptiles_of_Washington) and amphibians.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] Coastal bays and islands are often inhabited by plentiful amounts of shellfish and whales. There are five species of [salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmon) that ascend the Western Washington area, from streams to spawn.[^cite_note-Clark-109]

Washington has a variety of [National Park Service](161535.md) units. Among these are the [Alta Lake State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alta_Lake_State_Park), [Lake Roosevelt National Recreation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Roosevelt_National_Recreation_Area), [San Juan Islands National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan_Islands_National_Wildlife_Refuge), as well as three national parks—the [Olympic National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_National_Park), [North Cascades National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Cascades_National_Park), and [Mount Rainier National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Rainier_National_Park).[^cite_note-117] The three national parks were established between 1899 and 1968. Almost 95 percent (876,517 acres, 354,714 hectares, 3,547.14 square kilometers) of Olympic National Park's area has been designated as wilderness under the [National Wilderness Preservation System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Wilderness_Preservation_System).[^cite_note-118]

There are 143 [state parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_state_parks) and 9 [national forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_forest_\(United_States\)), run by the [Washington State Park System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Park_System) and the [United States Forest Service](42652.md).[^cite_note-119] The [Okanogan National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okanogan_National_Forest) is the largest national forest on the [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States), encompassing 1,499,023 acres (606,633 ha). It is managed together as the Okanogan–[Wenatchee National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wenatchee_National_Forest), encompassing a considerably larger area of around 3,239,404 acres (1,310,940 ha).[^cite_note-120]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [List of counties in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Washington) and [List of municipalities in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Washington)

There are 39 counties within Washington, and 281 incorporated municipalities which are divided into cities and towns.[^cite_note-121] The majority of the state's population lives within Western Washington, in the Seattle metropolitan area. The city of Seattle is the principal city of the metropolitan area, and Western Washington, with a 2020 census population of 737,015.[^cite_note-122]&#x20;

Washington State [population pyramid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_pyramid)

### Population

Washington's population was 7,705,281 in the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census),[^cite_note-Census2020-Tab2-128] a 14.6% increase since the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census).[^cite_note-PopEstUS-129] In 2020, the state ranked 13th overall in population, and was the third most populous, after California and Texas, west of the Mississippi River.[^cite_note-130] Washington has the largest population and highest population density among states in the Pacific Northwest, followed by Oregon and Idaho. The Washington State Office of Financial Management estimated the state population to be 8,115,100 as of April 1, 2025.[^cite_note-131]

The [Seattle–Tacoma–Bellevue metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_metropolitan_area)'s population was 4,018,762 in the 2020 census, more than half the state total.[^cite_note-132] The [center of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population) of Washington in 2010 was at [47°20′N 121°37′W  /  47.33°N 121.62°W  / 47.33; -121.62](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Washington_\(state\)\&params=47.33_N_121.62_W_), in an unpopulated part of the [Cascade Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Mountains) in rural eastern [King County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County), southeast of [North Bend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Bend,_Washington), northeast of [Enumclaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enumclaw), and west of [Snoqualmie Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snoqualmie_Pass).[^cite_note-133]

In 2020, Washington's proportion of residents under the age of five was 5.7%, 21.8% under 18, and 16.3% 65 or older.[^cite_note-2020DP1-134]

According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress), there were an estimated 25,211 [homeless people in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_in_Washington_\(state\)).[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] Data from a 2023 study was released by the [Washington State Office of Superintendent of Public Instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Office_of_Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction) (OSPI) which recorded 42,436 students in the state as meeting a variety of definitions to be described as homeless, affecting 3.8% of the student population.[^cite_note-137] A 2022-2023 report undertaken by a national, homeless advocacy group known as Schoolhouse Connections, listed 13,876 babies and toddlers as experiencing a form of homelessness, or approximately above 4% of the state's population in the age group.[^cite_note-138]

Four-fifths of the state's population identifies as White or European American. Washington has some of the largest Native American and Asian populations among states in the U.S.; the state also has a small proportion of [African Americans](2154.md). Washington's Hispanic community began growing rapidly in the late 20th century.[^cite_note-Clark-109] In 2018, the top countries of origin for Washington's immigrants were [Mexico](3966054.md), [India](14533.md), [China](5405.md), the [Philippines](23440.md) and [Vietnam](202354.md).[^cite_note-139] There are 29 [federally recognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_in_the_contiguous_United_States) Native American tribes in the state, mostly in Western Washington, and other unrecognized groups.[^cite_note-140]

In the 2016 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), 12.1% of Washington's population were of [Hispanic or Latino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) origin (of any race): [Mexican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans) (9.7%), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stateside_Puerto_Ricans) (0.4%), [Cuban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Americans) (0.1%), and other Hispanic or Latino origin (1.8%).[^cite_note-ACS2016DEMO-150] The five largest ancestry groups were: [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) (17.8%), [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) (10.8%), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Americans) (10.4%), [Norwegian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Americans) (5.4%), and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_ancestry) (4.6%).[^cite_note-151]

Birth data

In 2011, 44.3 percent of Washington's population younger than age 1 were minorities.[^cite_note-152]

### Areas of concentration



Washington population density map

African Americans in the Pacific Northwest are underrepresented relative to the U.S.'s total population. Washington's Black population is concentrated in the [South End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_End,_Seattle) and [Central District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_District,_Seattle) areas of Seattle, and in inner Tacoma.[^cite_note-164] The Black community of Seattle consisted of one individual in 1858, [Manuel Lopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Lopes_\(barber\)), and grew to a population of 406 by 1900.[^cite_note-165] It developed substantially during and after World War II when wartime industries and the [U.S. Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Armed_Forces) employed and recruited tens of thousands of African Americans from the [Southeastern United States](816925.md). They moved west in the second wave of the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), leaving a high influence on West Coast [rock music](25423.md) and [R\&B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R\&B) and [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_music) in the 1960s, including Seattle native [Jimi Hendrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimi_Hendrix), a pioneer in hard rock, who was of African-American and alleged [Cherokee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherokee) descent.

[Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) lived on Indian reservations or jurisdiction lands such as the [Colville Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colville_Indian_Reservation) (including several [Interior Salish tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Salish_tribes), [Makah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makah_Reservation), [Muckleshoot Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muckleshoot_Indian_Reservation), [Quinault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinault_people), [Spokane Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spokane_Indian_Reservation), and [Yakama Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakama_Indian_Reservation). The westernmost and Pacific coasts have primarily American Indian communities, such as the [Chinook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinookan_peoples), [Lummi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lummi_people), and [Coast Salish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast_Salish_peoples). [Urban Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Indian) communities formed by the U.S. [Bureau of Indian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Indian_Affairs) relocation programs in Seattle since the end of World War II brought a variety of Native American peoples to this diverse metropolis. The city was named for [Chief Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Seattle) in the very early 1850s when European Americans settled the sound.



[Chinese New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_New_Year), Seattle (2011)

Asian Americans are mostly concentrated in the Seattle−Tacoma metropolitan area. Seattle, [Bellevue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellevue,_Washington), and [Redmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmond,_Washington), which are all in King County, have sizable Chinese communities (including [Taiwanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_Americans)), as well as significant [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_American) and [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Americans) communities. The [Chinatown–International District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown%E2%80%93International_District) in Seattle has a historical Chinese population dating back to the 1860s, who mainly emigrated from [Guangdong](65109.md) Province in southern China, and is home to a diverse East and Southeast Asian community.

[Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Americans) are heavily concentrated in the suburban cities of [Federal Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Way) and [Auburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auburn,_Washington) to the south, and in [Lynnwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynnwood) to the north. Tacoma is home to thousands of [Cambodians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian_Americans), and has one of the largest Cambodian-American communities in the United States, along with [Long Beach, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Beach,_California), and [Lowell, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowell,_Massachusetts).[^cite_note-166] The [Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnamese_Americans) and [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Americans) populations of Washington are mostly concentrated within the Seattle metropolitan area.[^cite_note-167]

Washington state has the second highest percentage of Pacific Islander people in the mainland U.S. (behind [Utah](31716.md)). In 2009, the Seattle–Tacoma area was home to more than 15,000 people of [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_Americans) ancestry, who mainly reside in southeast Seattle, Tacoma, Federal Way, and in [SeaTac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SeaTac,_Washington).[^cite_note-pugetsoundsamoan-168][^cite_note-factfinder2.census.gov-169]

The most numerous ethnic (not racial) group is Latinos, who comprise 11% of Washington's population. [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_American) formed a large ethnic group in the [Chehalis Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chehalis,_Washington), [Skagit Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skagit_County), farming areas of [Yakima Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakima_River), and [Eastern Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Washington). They were reported to at least date as far back as the 1800s.[^cite_note-170] But it was in the late 20th century, that large-scale Mexican immigration and other Latinos settled in the southern suburbs of Seattle, with limited concentrations in King, [Pierce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierce_County,_Washington), and [Snohomish Counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snohomish_County,_Washington) during the region's real estate construction booms in the 1980s and 1990s.

Washington has a large [Ethiopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Americans) community, with many [Eritrean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_Americans) residents as well.[^cite_note-171] Both emerged in the late 1960s, and developed since 1980.[^cite_note-172] In 2010, an estimated 30,000 [Somali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somali_Americans) immigrants lived in the Seattle area.[^cite_note-173]

In 2010, 82.51% (5,060,313) of Washington residents age 5 and older spoke English at home as a [primary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_language). 7.79% (477,566) spoke Spanish, 1.19% (72,552) Chinese (which includes [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese) and [Standard Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chinese)), 0.94% (57,895) Vietnamese, 0.84% (51,301) [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language), 0.83% (50,757) Korean, 0.80% (49,282) Russian, and 0.55% (33,744) German. In total, 17.49% (1,073,002) of Washington's population age 5 and older spoke a [mother language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_language) other than English.[^cite_note-MLA_Data-174]
