In 2010, the largest denominations by number of adherents were the Roman [Catholic Church](606848.md), with 784,332; [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_Washington_\(state\)), with 282,356;[^cite_note-178] and the [Assemblies of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblies_of_God), with 125,005.[^cite_note-179]

[Aquarian Tabernacle Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquarian_Tabernacle_Church) is the largest [Wiccan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wicca) church in the country.[^cite_note-180]

Like other West Coast states, the percentage of Washington's population identifying themselves as "[non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-religious)" is higher than the national average.

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Washington_\(state\))

See also: [List of Washington locations by per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_locations_by_per_capita_income) and [List of Washington companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_companies)



[Microsoft](19001.md) Corporation headquarters in [Redmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmond,_Washington), an [Eastside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastside_\(King_County,_Washington\)) suburb of Seattle

According to the [Bureau of Economic Analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Economic_Analysis), in 2025, the State of Washington's [gross state product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_state_product) was \$894.9 billion and its per capita personal income was \$89,396.[^cite_note-181] As of June 2025, the state's [unemployment rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_rate) was 4.5 percent, ranked 37th lowest in the [United States](3434750.md);[^cite_note-182] the rate is unchanged from June 2024.[^cite_note-june25employment-183]

In the late 2010s, the state had the fastest-growing economy in the United States and was tenth-largest in the nation.[^cite_note-184] The [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) was set at \$11 in 2017 and has increased annually based on a [cost-of-living index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost-of-living_index); since January 1, 2024, it has been \$16.28 an hour, the highest of any state.[^cite_note-185] Several cities have higher minimum wages as of 2024, such as Seattle at \$19.97 for large employers and [Tukwila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tukwila,_Washington) at \$20.29 for large employers.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

In 2025, [small businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_businesses) made up 99.5% of the businesses in Washington and employed 48.7% of its work force.[^cite_note-188] In 2024, based on a study by the [National Partnership for Women and Families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Partnership_for_Women_and_Families), annual wages for women in the state were \$18,545 less than men, the second-largest income gap in the United States. The average gap was an increase from 2023, reported to be \$17,400.[^cite_note-189]

Significant business within the state include the design and manufacture of aircraft ([Boeing](18933266.md)), [automotive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive) ([Paccar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paccar)), computer software development ([Microsoft](19001.md), [Bungie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bungie), [Amazon](90451.md), [Nintendo of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nintendo), [Valve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valve_Corporation), [ArenaNet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ArenaNet), [Cyan Worlds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyan_Worlds)), [telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_service_provider) ([T-Mobile US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Mobile_US)), [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics), [biotechnology](4502.md), [aluminum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aluminum) production, lumber and wood products ([Weyerhaeuser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weyerhaeuser)), mining, beverages ([Starbucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starbucks), [Jones Soda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Soda)), real estate ([John L. Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_L._Scott), [Colliers International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colliers_International), [Windermere Real Estate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windermere_Real_Estate), Kidder Mathews), retail ([Nordstrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordstrom), [Eddie Bauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Bauer), Car Toys, [Costco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costco), [R.E.I.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R.E.I.)), and tourism ([Alaska Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Airlines), [Expedia, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedia,_Inc.)). A *[Fortune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_\(magazine\))* magazine survey of the top 20 Most Admired Companies in the U.S. has four Washington-based companies: Amazon, Starbucks, Microsoft, and Costco.[^cite_note-190] At over 80 percent the state has significant amounts of hydroelectric power generation. Significant amounts of trade with Asia pass through the ports of the Puget Sound, leading to a number six ranking of U.S. ports. Ranking combines twenty-foot equivalent units (TEUs) moved and infrastructure index.[^cite_note-191]

With the passage of Initiative 1183, the [Washington State Liquor Control Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Liquor_Control_Board) (WSLCB) ended its monopoly of all-state liquor store and liquor distribution operations on June 1, 2012. The board transitioned into licensing and regulating the sale of alcohol, tobacco, and later [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis) after the passage of [Initiative 502](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiative_502).[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

### Taxes

| icon | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability)**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Washington_\(state\)) by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Referencing_for_beginners). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(July 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

The state of Washington is one of seven states that do not levy a personal [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax). However, in March 2026, Washington governor [Bob Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ferguson_\(politician\)) signed [Senate Bill 6346](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millionaires_tax_\(Washington_state\)) into law, establishing the state's first income tax on household income exceeding \$1 million per year, effective January 1, 2028.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

The state does not collect a [corporate income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_income_tax) or [franchise tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franchise_tax) either. Washington businesses are responsible for various other state levies, including the [business and occupation tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_and_occupation_tax) (B & O), a [gross receipts tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_receipts_tax) which charges varying rates for different types of businesses.

Washington's state base [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax) is 6.5%, which is combined with a local sales tax that varies by locality. The combined state and local retail sales tax rates increase the taxes paid by consumers, depending on the variable local sales tax rates, generally between 7.5% and 10%.[^cite_note-DORSalesTaxRates-196] As of 2024, the combined sales tax rate in Seattle was 10.25%. The Snohomish County cities of [Lynnwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynnwood), [Mill Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mill_Creek,_Washington), [Mukilteo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukilteo) are tied for the highest sales tax rate in the state at 10.6%.[^cite_note-197] These taxes apply to services as well as products, but not most foods due to a 1977 ballot measure.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] However, prepared foods, [dietary supplements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dietary_supplement), and [soft drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_drink) remain taxable.[^cite_note-200]

An [excise tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excise_tax) applies to certain products such as gasoline, cigarettes, and alcoholic beverages. [Property tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax) was the first tax levied in the state of Washington, and its collection accounts for about 30% of Washington's total state and local revenue. It continues to be the most important revenue source for [public schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_school_\(government-funded\)), fire protection, [libraries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libraries), parks and recreation, and other special-purpose districts.

All [real property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_property) and [personal property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_property) are subject to tax unless specifically exempted by law. Most personal property owned by individuals is exempt from tax. [Personal property tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_property_tax) applies to personal property used when conducting business, or to other personal property not exempt by law. All property taxes are paid to the county treasurer's office where the property is located. Neither does the state assess any tax on retirement income earned and received from another state. Washington does not collect [inheritance taxes](5824627.md). However, the [estate tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estate_tax) is de-coupled from the federal estate tax laws, and therefore, the state imposes its estate tax.

Washington state has the 18th highest per capita [effective tax rate in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_tax_levels_in_the_United_States), as of 2017. As of June 2023, Washington has the highest gasoline prices in the United States, at an average of \$4.97, in part due to the third-highest gasoline tax in the country.[^cite_note-201] Their tax policy differs from neighboring Oregon's, which levies no sales tax, but does levy a personal income tax. This leads to [border economic anomalies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border#Border_economics) in the [Portland–Vancouver metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland%E2%80%93Vancouver_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-202] Additional border economies with tax disparities exist with neighboring Idaho, which has a lower sales tax rate;[^cite_note-203] and British Columbia, which has higher costs for goods and has residents who commute into Washington for shopping.[^cite_note-204] These include remote mailbox and courier services for American online retailers, which became ubiquitous in border communities in the 21st century.[^cite_note-205]

Washington has the highest liquor tax in the United States.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] The spirits tax is such that it disproportionately impacts lower alcohol content cocktails containing distilled spirits as they're taxed the same based on volume and price regardless of alcohol percentage.[^cite_note-209]

### Agriculture



[Azwell, WA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azwell,_Washington), a small community of [pickers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruit_picking)' cabins and apple [orchards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchard)

Washington is a leading agricultural state. In 2023, the total value of Washington's agricultural products was \$14 billion.[^cite_note-agr.wa.gov-11] In 2014, Washington ranked first in the nation in production of red [raspberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raspberries) (90.5 percent of total U.S. production), [hops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hops) (79.3 percent), [spearmint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spearmint) oil (75 percent), wrinkled seed [peas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pea) (70.4 percent), [apples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apple) (71.1 percent), sweet [cherries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherries) (62.3 percent), [pears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pear) (45.6 percent), [Concord grapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concord_grape) (55.1 percent), [carrots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrot) for processing (30.6 percent), and green peas for processing (32.4 percent).[^cite_note-210]

Washington also ranked second in the nation in the production of fall potatoes (a quarter of the nation's production), [nectarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nectarine), [apricots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apricot), asparagus, all raspberries, grapes (all varieties taken together), sweet corn for processing (a quarter of the nation's production), and summer onions (a fifth of the nation's production). Washington also ranked third in the nation in the production of dried peas, lentils, onions, and peppermint oil.[^cite_note-agr.wa.gov-11]

The apple industry is of particular importance to Washington. Because of the favorable climate of dry, warm summers and cold winters of central Washington, the state has led the U.S. in apple production since the 1920s.[^cite_note-211] Two areas account for the vast majority of the state's apple crop: the Wenatchee–Okanogan region (comprising [Chelan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelan_County,_Washington), [Okanogan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okanogan_County,_Washington), [Douglas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_County,_Washington), and [Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_County,_Washington) counties), and the Yakima region (comprising [Yakima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakima_County,_Washington), [Benton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benton_County,_Washington), and [Kittitas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kittitas_County,_Washington) counties).[^cite_note-212] Washington produces seven principal varieties of apples which are exported to more than sixty countries.[^cite_note-213]

### Wine

Main article: [Washington wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_wine)



[Rattlesnake Hills AVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattlesnake_Hills_AVA), one of nineteen [American Viticultural Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Viticultural_Area) in the state

Washington ranks third [in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wine_of_the_United_States) in the production of wine, behind [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_wine) and [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_wine), having produced 25.7 million U.S. gallons (97 million liters) in 2024.[^cite_note-ttb-214]

In 2006, the state had over 31,000 acres (130 km<sup>2</sup>) of [vineyards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vineyard), a [harvest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest_\(wine\)) of 120,000 short tons (109,000 metric tons) of grapes, and exports going to more than forty countries around the world from the state's 600 [wineries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wineries). By 2021, that number had grown to 1,050 wineries. While there are some [viticultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viticultural) activities in the cooler, wetter [western half](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Washington) of the state, almost all (99%) of wine grape production takes place in the desert-like [eastern half](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Washington).[^cite_note-Oxford_pg_761-762-215] The [rain shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow) of the Cascade Range leaves the [Columbia River Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_River_Basin) with around 8 inches (200 mm) of annual rain fall, making [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation_in_viticulture) and [water rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_right) of paramount interest to the Washington wine industry. Viticulture in the state is also influenced by long sunlight hours (on average, two more hours a day than in California during the [growing season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growing_season)) and consistent temperatures.[^cite_note-Fallis_pg_50-216]

### Military

As of 2022, Washington has 108,542 total [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md) personnel, including active duty members of the military and civilian workers at [United States Armed Forces](32212.md) bases.[^cite_note-DOD-Spending-217] It ranks seventh among states for most active duty personnel, at over 60,000, and seventeenth for [reserve members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_components_of_the_United_States_Armed_Forces).[^cite_note-218] The [U.S. Navy](20518076.md) and [Marines](17349325.md) comprise the largest branch in Washington with 45 percent of personnel, followed by the [Army](32087.md) at 40 percent and the [Air Force](32090.md) at 11 percent.[^cite_note-DOD-Spending-217] The state is also home to the 11th-largest population of retirees and veterans at over 560,000 as of 2019.[^cite_note-219]

The state's largest military installations are centered around the Puget Sound region and include [Joint Base Lewis–McChord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Base_Lewis%E2%80%93McChord) in Pierce County, the largest military base on the West Coast with over 25,000 active duty soldiers;[^cite_note-220] [Naval Station Everett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Station_Everett) in Snohomish County; and [Naval Air Station Whidbey Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Air_Station_Whidbey_Island) in Island County.[^cite_note-DOD-Spending-217][^cite_note-Talton-Military-221] The Kitsap Peninsula is home to [Naval Base Kitsap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Base_Kitsap), which includes the [Puget Sound Naval Shipyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puget_Sound_Naval_Shipyard) in Bremerton and [Naval Submarine Base Bangor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_Submarine_Base_Bangor),[^cite_note-Talton-Military-221] site of the third-largest arsenal of [nuclear weapons](21785.md) in the world with more than 1,100 warheads for submarines.[^cite_note-222] [Fairchild Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairchild_Air_Force_Base) is a major air force installation near Spokane that has the largest [aerial refueling](237949.md) fleet in the world.[^cite_note-223] Washington also has several major companies that serve as [defense contractors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_defense_contractors) for the U.S. military who were awarded \$6.9 billion in [fiscal year](5752099.md) 2022. The largest contractors in the state include Boeing, [PacMed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PacMed?action=edit\&redlink=1), and Microsoft.[^cite_note-DOD-Spending-217][^cite_note-224]

### Internet access

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Washington_\(state\)\&action=edit\&section=). *(December 2017)* |
| --- | --- |

From 2009 to 2014, the Washington State Broadband Project was awarded \$7.3 million in federal grants, but the program was discontinued in 2014.[^cite_note-225] For infrastructure, another \$166 million has been awarded since 2011 for broadband infrastructure projects in Washington state.[^cite_note-226]

*[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report)&#x20;*&#x20;ranked Washington second nationally for household internet access, and sixth for online download speed, based on data from 2014 and 2015.[^cite_note-227]

In 2019, Washington State Legislature established the Washington State Broadband Office with two key mandates: high-speed internet access for 100% of WA residents by 2024 and an increase to 150/150 Mbit/s by 2028.

In March 2021, the Washington State Department of Commerce issued their first biennial report on the progress of these key mandates throughout 2020.[^cite_note-228]

The report includes five sections: public survey results, digital adoption disparities as they relate to federal census data, a Partner-Plan-Fund-Build-Adopt model for continued progress, success stories, and a policy discussion conclusion.

According to the report, "...over 42,000 survey responses from nearly 32,000 unique locations, showing that 6.4 percent of respondents reported having no broadband service, and 57 percent reported service at download speeds under 25 Mbps..."

## Transportation

Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence). *(November 2016)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |



The [Washington State Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Ferries) owns the largest ferry system in the United States.



[Floating bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontoon_bridge) on [Lake Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Washington). These are among the largest of their kind in the world.

Washington's state transportation system comprises several modes that are maintained by various government entities. The [state highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_highway) system, called [State Routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_state_routes_in_Washington), includes over 7,000 miles (11,000 km) of roads and the [Washington State Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Ferries) system, the largest of its kind in the nation[^cite_note-229] and the third largest in the world. There are also 57,200 miles (92,100 km) of local roads maintained by cities and counties, as well as several ferries operated by local governments.[^cite_note-WSTC-Presentation-230] There are 140 [public airfields in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Washington_\(state\)), including 16 state airports owned by the [Washington State Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Department_of_Transportation). [Seattle–Tacoma International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle%E2%80%93Tacoma_International_Airport) (Sea–Tac) is the major commercial airport of greater Seattle.[^cite_note-231] [Boeing Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_Field) in Seattle is one of the busiest primary non-hub airports in the U.S.[^cite_note-232]

There are extensive waterways around Washington's largest cities, including Seattle, [Bellevue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellevue,_Washington), Tacoma, and [Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia,_Washington). The state highways incorporate an extensive network of bridges and the largest ferry system in the United States to serve transportation needs in the Puget Sound area. Washington's marine highway constitutes a fleet of twenty-eight ferries that navigate [Puget Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puget_Sound) and its inland waterways to 20 different ports of call, completing close to 147,000 sailings each year.

Washington is home to four of the five longest [floating bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontoon_bridge) in the world: the [Evergreen Point Floating Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evergreen_Point_Floating_Bridge), [Lacey V. Murrow Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacey_V._Murrow_Memorial_Bridge) and [Homer M. Hadley Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer_M._Hadley_Memorial_Bridge) over [Lake Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Washington), and the [Hood Canal Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hood_Canal_Bridge) which connects the Olympic Peninsula and [Kitsap Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitsap_Peninsula). Among its most famous bridges is the [Tacoma Narrows Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma_Narrows_Bridge), which collapsed in 1940 and was rebuilt.

Washington has 75 port districts,[^cite_note-WSTC-Presentation-230] including several major [seaports on the Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_and_harbors_of_the_Pacific_Ocean). Among these are ports in [Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Seattle), [Tacoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Tacoma), [Kalama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalama,_Washington), [Anacortes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anacortes), [Vancouver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Vancouver_USA), [Everett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Everett), [Longview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Longview), Grays Harbor, Olympia, and [Port Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Angeles). The Columbia and Snake rivers also provide 465 miles (748 km) of inland waterways that are navigable by barges as far east as [Lewiston, Idaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewiston,_Idaho).[^cite_note-WSTC-Presentation-230][^cite_note-233]

The Cascade Mountain Range also impedes transportation. Washington operates and maintains roads over seven major [mountain passes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_pass) and eight minor passes. During the winter months, some of these passes are plowed, sanded, and kept safe with avalanche control. Not all stay open through the winter. The North Cascades Highway, [State Route 20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Route_20), closes every year due to snowfall and avalanches in the area of [Washington Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Pass). The [Cayuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cayuse_Pass) and [Chinook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_Pass) passes east of Mount Rainier also close in winter.[^cite_note-234]

Washington is crossed by [several freight railroads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_\(state\)_railroads), and [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak)'s passenger [Cascade route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak_Cascades) between Eugene, Oregon, and Vancouver, BC is the eighth busiest Amtrak service in the U.S. Seattle's [King Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Street_Station), the busiest station in Washington, and the 15th busiest in the U.S.,[^cite_note-235] serves as the terminus for the two long-distance Amtrak routes in Washington, the [Empire Builder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_Builder) to Chicago and the [Coast Starlight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast_Starlight) to Los Angeles. The [Sounder commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sounder_commuter_rail) service operates in Seattle and its surrounding cities, between [Everett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everett,_Washington) and [Lakewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakewood,_Washington). The intercity network includes the [Cascade Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Tunnel), the longest railroad tunnel in the United States, which is part of the [Stevens Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stevens_Pass) route on the BNSF Northern Transcom.[^cite_note-236]

Sound Transit [Link light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Link_light_rail) currently operates in the Seattle area at a length of 58 miles (93 km), and in [Tacoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma,_Washington) at a length of 4 miles (6.4 km). The entire system has a funded expansion plan that will expand light rail to a total of 116 miles by 2041. Seattle also has a 3.8-mile (6.1 km) [streetcar network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Streetcar) with two lines and plans to expand further by 2025. 32 local bus transit systems exist across the state,[^cite_note-WSTC-Presentation-230] the busiest being [King County Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County_Metro), located in Seattle and King County, with just above 122 million riders in 2017.[^cite_note-237] Clark County has historically resisted proposals to extend Portland's [MAX Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MAX_Light_Rail) into Vancouver, including the rejection of two ballot measures, but light rail is slated to be included in a future replacement of the [Interstate Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Bridge).[^cite_note-238]

Some tribal governments offer [free bus service](11163017.md) on their respective reservations, including on the [Muckleshoot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muckleshoot),[^cite_note-239] [Spokane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spokane_Indian_Reservation),[^cite_note-240] and [Yakama Indian Reservations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakama_Indian_Reservation).[^cite_note-241]

## Environment

[Hanford Nuclear Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanford_Nuclear_Reservation) is considered one of the most [contaminated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_contamination) nuclear sites in the United States[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-ecoHanford-243] and is the focus of the nation's largest [environmental cleanup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_cleanup).[^cite_note-244] The radioactive materials are known to be leaking from Hanford into the environment.[^cite_note-245] Another major cleanup site is the [Duwamish River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duwamish_River) basin in Seattle, among the most contaminated bodies of water in the United States due to industrial runoff.[^cite_note-246]

In 2007, Washington became the first state in the nation to target all forms of highly toxic [brominated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bromine) [flame retardants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flame_retardant) known as [PBDEs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBDE) for elimination from the many common household products in which they are being used. A 2004 study of 40 mothers from Oregon, Washington, British Columbia, and Montana found PBDEs in the breast milk of every woman tested.

Three recent studies by the [Washington State Department of Ecology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Department_of_Ecology) showed toxic chemicals banned decades ago linger in the environment and concentrate in the food chain. In one of the studies, state government scientists found unacceptable levels of toxic substances in 93 samples of freshwater fish from 45 sites. The toxic substances included [PCBs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PCBs), [dioxins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polychlorinated_dibenzodioxins), two chlorinated pesticides, [DDE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dichlorodiphenyldichloroethylene), [dieldrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dieldrin) and PBDEs. As a result of the study, the department will investigate the sources of PCBs in the Wenatchee River, where unhealthy levels of PCBs were found in mountain whitefish. Based on the 2007 information and a previous 2004 Ecology study, the [Washington State Department of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Department_of_Health) advises the public not to eat [mountain whitefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_whitefish) from the [Wenatchee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wenatchee_River) from [Leavenworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leavenworth,_Washington) downstream to where the river joins the Columbia, due to unhealthy levels of PCBs. Study results also showed high levels of contaminants in fish tissue that scientists collected from Lake Washington and the Spokane River, where fish consumption advisories are already in effect.[^cite_note-247]

In March 2006, Governor [Christine Gregoire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christine_Gregoire) signed into law the recently approved House Bill 2322. This bill would limit [phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorus) content in dishwashing detergents statewide to 0.5 percent over the next six years. Although the ban took effect statewide in 2010, it was implemented earlier in Whatcom, Spokane, and Clark counties in 2008.[^cite_note-248] A recent discovery had linked high contents of phosphorus in water to a boom in [algae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algae) population. An invasive amount of algae in bodies of water would lead to a variety of excess ecological and technological issues.[^cite_note-249]

## Utilities

In 2023, the electricity sold by public and private suppliers for use in Washington was primarily sourced from [hydroelectric dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_dam) (49%), followed by [natural gas](22131.md) (12%), [wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power) (10%), coal (6.5%), and nuclear (4%). A total of 89.3 million [Megawatt-hours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawatt-hour) of electricity was generated statewide in 2023.[^cite_note-250] As of 2025, Washington generates the most hydroelectric power of all U.S. states and accounts for approximately 25% of the nation's total hydroelectric generation capability. Its percentage of energy from all [renewable sources](25784.md) is third among U.S. states, behind Vermont and South Dakota.[^cite_note-251]

## Government and politics

### State government

Main article: [Government of Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Washington_\(state\))



The [Washington State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Capitol) building in [Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia,_Washington)

Washington's [executive branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_branch) is headed by a governor elected for a four-year term. The current statewide elected officials are:

- [Bob Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ferguson_\(politician\)), [Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Washington) (D)

- [Denny Heck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_Heck), [Lieutenant Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Washington) (D)

- [Steve Hobbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Hobbs_\(Washington_politician\)), [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Washington) (D)

- [Mike Pellicciotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pellicciotti), [State Treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Treasurer) (D)

- [Patrice McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_McCarthy_\(politician\)), [State Auditor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Auditor) (D)

- [Nick Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_W._Brown_\(lawyer\)), [Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_Washington) (D)

- [Chris Reykdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Reykdal), [Superintendent of Public Instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Office_of_Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction) (NP/D)

  [^cite_note-252]

- [Dave Upthegrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Upthegrove), [Commissioner of Public Lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Department_of_Natural_Resources) (D)

- [Patty Kuderer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patty_Kuderer), [Insurance Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Office_of_the_Insurance_Commissioner) (D)

The [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral) [Washington State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Legislature) is the state's [legislative branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_branch). The [state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_legislature_\(United_States\)) is composed of a [lower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house) [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_House_of_Representatives) and an [upper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house) [State Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Senate). The state is divided into 49 legislative districts of equal population, each of which elects two representatives and one senator. Representatives serve two-year terms, while senators serve for four years. There are no [term limits](505825.md). The [Democratic Party](5043544.md) has a majority in the House and Senate.

The [Washington Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Supreme_Court) is the highest court in the state and meets in Olympia. Nine justices serve on the bench and are elected statewide or appointed by the governor to fill vacancies.[^cite_note-253] There are 30 judicial districts, each with a [superior court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superior_court); these districts roughly correspond to counties, with some districts that combine rural or closely related counties.[^cite_note-254]

### Federal representation

See also: [Washington's congressional delegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington's_congressional_delegations)

Two adult women talk with an older white-haired man in camouflage inside a dark room.

U.S. Senators [Patty Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patty_Murray) and [Maria Cantwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Cantwell) visit [Fairchild Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairchild_Air_Force_Base).

The two current [United States senators from Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_senators_from_Washington) are [Patty Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patty_Murray) and [Maria Cantwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Cantwell), both Democrats. Murray has represented the state since 1993, while Cantwell assumed office in 2001. The state is one of four with two female senators.[^cite_note-255]

Washington's ten representatives in the United States House of Representatives ([*see map of districts*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington's_congressional_districts)) as of the 2022 election are [Suzan DelBene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzan_DelBene) (D-1), [Rick Larsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Larsen) (D-2), [Marie Gluesenkamp Perez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Gluesenkamp_Perez) (D-3), [Dan Newhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Newhouse) (R-4), [Michael Baumgartner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Baumgartner) (R-5), [Emily Randall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emily_Randall) (D-6), [Pramila Jayapal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pramila_Jayapal) (D-7), [Kim Schrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Schrier) (D-8), [Adam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Smith_\(Washington_politician\)) (D-9), and [Marilyn Strickland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marilyn_Strickland) (D-10).

Due to [Congressional redistricting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_redistricting) as a result of the [2010 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census), Washington gained one seat in the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md). With the extra seat, Washington also gained one electoral vote, raising its total to 12.

A [treemap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treemap) of the popular vote by county, 2016 presidential election

The state is typically thought of as politically divided by the Cascade Mountains, with Western Washington being [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States) (particularly the [I-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_5_in_Washington) Corridor) and Eastern Washington being [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States).

Although the eastern half of the state votes heavily [Republican](32070.md), the overwhelming [Democratic](5043544.md) dominance in the [Seattle metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_metropolitan_area) has turned Washington into a reliably [blue state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_state). It is considered part of the [Blue wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_wall_\(United_States\)) of states that have voted Democratic in every presidential election since [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election). This voting streak began with Democrat [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis) narrowly capturing Washington in [1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_United_States_presidential_election). The state has since turned much more solidly blue, beginning with Obama's landslide victory in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), and Democrats winning the state by double digits in every subsequent presidential election. In [2024](59568546.md), Washington had the smallest swing to the right of any state, swinging by just 1%, compared to the national swing of about 6%.[^cite_note-263]

Washington was considered a key swing state in 1968, and it was the only western state to give its electoral votes to Democratic nominee [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey) over his Republican opponent [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon). Washington was considered a part of the 1994 [Republican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Revolution), and had the biggest pick-up in the house for Republicans, who picked up seven of Washington's nine House seats.[^cite_note-264] However, this dominance did not last for long, as Democrats picked up one seat in the 1996 election,[^cite_note-265] and two more in 1998, giving the Democrats a 5–4 majority.[^cite_note-266]

In 2013 and 2014, both houses of the [Washington State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Legislature) (the [Washington Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Senate) and the [Washington House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_House_of_Representatives)) were controlled by Democrats. The state senate was under Republican control, due to two Democrats' joining Republicans to form the [Majority Coalition Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_Coalition_Caucus). After the 2014 elections, the Democrats retained control of the House, while Republicans took a majority in the Senate without the need for a coalition. In November 2017, a special election gave Democrats a one-seat majority in the Senate and complete control over state government. Since then, in the 2018 election, the Democrats have only expanded their majorities.

The [governorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Washington) is currently held by Democrat [Bob Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ferguson_\(politician\)). No state has gone longer without a Republican governor than Washington. Democrats have controlled the [Washington Governor's Mansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Governor's_Mansion) for 41 years; the last Republican governor was [John Spellman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Spellman), who left office in 1985. Washington has not voted for a Republican senator, governor, or presidential candidate since 1994, tying with Delaware for the longest streak in the country.[^cite_note-267]

Washington uses the [non-partisan blanket primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpartisan_blanket_primary) system after the approval of [Initiative 872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiative_872) in 2004.[^cite_note-268] All candidates run on the same ballot during primary elections and the top two candidates advance to the general election in November, regardless of party affiliation. This has resulted in several same-party general election match-ups. In a 2020 study, Washington was ranked as the second easiest state for citizens to vote in.[^cite_note-269]

The 2023 *American Values Atlas* by the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute) found that [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) is supported near-universally in Washington.[^cite_note-270]

### Notable legislation



The [Suzzallo Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzzallo_Library) reading room at the state's flagship University of Washington

Washington is one of the ten states to have legalized [assisted suicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_suicide). In 2008, the [Washington Death with Dignity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Death_with_Dignity_Act) ballot initiative passed and became law.[^cite_note-271]

In November 2009, Washington voters approved full domestic partnerships via [Referendum 71](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum_71), marking the first time voters in any state expanded recognition of same-sex relationships at the ballot box. Three years later, in November 2012, [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Washington_\(state\)) was affirmed via [Referendum 74](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum_74), making Washington one of only three states to have approved same-sex marriage by popular vote.

In November 2012, Washington was one of the first two states to approve the legal sale and possession of [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis) for both recreational and medical use with [Initiative 502](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiative_502). Although marijuana is still illegal under U.S. federal law, persons 21 and older in Washington state can possess up to one ounce of marijuana, 16 ounces of marijuana-infused product in solid form, 72 ounces of marijuana-infused product in liquid form, or any combination of all three, and can legally consume marijuana and marijuana-infused products.[^cite_note-272]

In November 2016, voters approved Initiative 1433, which among other things requires employers to guarantee paid [sick leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sick_leave) to most workers. On January 1, 2018, the law went into effect, with Washington becoming the seventh state with paid sick leave requirements.[^cite_note-273]

With the passage of [Initiative 1639](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiative_1639) in the 2018 elections, Washington adopted stricter gun laws.

Washington enacted a measure in May 2019 in favor of [sanctuary cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_cities), similar to California and Oregon laws which are among the strongest statewide mandates in the nation.[^cite_note-274]

In 2019, the legislature passed the Clean Energy Transformation Act, which requires all electricity sales to be from zero-carbon sources by 2045 and net-zero by 2030.[^cite_note-275]

## Education

### Elementary and secondary education

See also: [List of school districts in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_school_districts_in_Washington) and [List of high schools in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_schools_in_Washington_\(state\))

As of the 2024–2025 school year, 1.1 million students were enrolled in elementary and secondary schools in Washington. 68 thousand teachers were employed in 2023–2024 and the cost to educate each pupil was \$18,649.[^cite_note-276]

As of August 2009, there were 295 [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district) in the state, serviced by nine [Educational Service Districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_service_district_\(Washington\)).[^cite_note-277] [Washington School Information Processing Cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_School_Information_Processing_Cooperative) (a non-profit opt-in state agency) provides information management systems for fiscal and human resources and student data. Elementary and secondary schools are under the jurisdiction of the [Washington State Office of Superintendent of Public Instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Office_of_Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction) (OSPI).[^cite_note-278]

High school [juniors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleventh_grade) and [seniors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_\(education\)) in Washington have the option of using the state's [Running Start](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_Start) program. Begun by the [state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Legislature) in 1990, it allows students to attend institutions of higher education at public expense, simultaneously earning high school and college credit.[^cite_note-279] The state has 141 schools that offer [dual language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_language) programs in 14 languages, primarily Spanish, beginning in kindergarten.[^cite_note-280]

Washington has several public arts-focused high schools including [Tacoma School of the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma_School_of_the_Arts), the [Vancouver School of Arts and Academics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver_School_of_Arts_and_Academics), and [The Center School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Center_School_\(Seattle\)). There are five science- and math-based high schools: one in the [Tri-Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tri-Cities,_Washington) known as Delta, one in Tacoma known as SAMI, another in Seattle known as [Raisbeck Aviation High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raisbeck_Aviation_High_School), one in Redmond known as [Tesla STEM High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_STEM_High_School), and one in Vancouver known as [Vancouver iTech Preparatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver_iTech_Preparatory).

### Higher education

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of colleges and universities in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Washington_\(state\)).

In 2023, [U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report) ranked Washington state at No. 2 in its ranking of "Best States for Higher Education," taking into consideration the share of the population with undergraduate degrees, graduation rates across colleges and universities, as well as the costs associated with studying in each state.[^cite_note-281] There are more than 40 institutions of higher education in Washington. The state has major research universities, technical schools, religious schools, and private career colleges.

Washington has six public four-year universities.[^cite_note-public_colleges-282] The [flagship school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagship#University) of Washington's university system is the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) and its [land-grant institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land-grant_university) is [Washington State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_University), each of which have been designated as "R1: Doctoral Universities – Very high research activity" schools by the [Carnegie Classification of Institutions of Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Classification_of_Institutions_of_Higher_Education).[^cite_note-carnegie_class-283] The other four public universities are [Western Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Washington_University), [Eastern Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Washington_University), [Central Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Washington_University), and the [Evergreen State College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evergreen_State_College), a public [liberal arts college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_arts_college).

Major private four-year colleges and universities include the [Jesuit colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Jesuit_Colleges_and_Universities) [Seattle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_University) and [Gonzaga University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonzaga_University), Christian schools [Seattle Pacific University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Pacific_University) and [Pacific Lutheran University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Lutheran_University), as well as smaller liberal arts colleges like [Annapolis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapolis_Group) members [Whitman College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitman_College) and the [University of Puget Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Puget_Sound).

The largest two-year college system in Washington is that of the [Seattle Colleges District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Colleges_District), which is the state's second-largest institution of higher education by total enrollment after the University of Washington.[^cite_note-hill-press-release-01-284]

## Media

See also: [List of newspapers in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Washington_\(state\)), [List of television stations in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_stations_in_Washington_\(state\)), and [List of radio stations in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Washington_\(state\))



The former offices of the *[Seattle Post-Intelligencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Post-Intelligencer)*, a major daily newspaper

As of 2022, Washington has 20 daily newspapers and 96 weekly newspapers that serve local and hyperlocal markets.[^cite_note-LWV-Newspapers-285] The most-circulated newspaper in the state is *[The Seattle Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seattle_Times)*, which is also among the most-circulated newspapers in the United States.[^cite_note-286] Other major daily newspapers include *[The Spokesman-Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spokesman-Review)* in Spokane, *[The News Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_News_Tribune)* in Tacoma, *[The Columbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Columbian)* in Vancouver, *[The Daily Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Everett_Herald)* in Everett, the *[Tri-City Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tri-City_Herald)* in Kennewick, and the *[Kitsap Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitsap_Sun)* in Bremerton.[^cite_note-LWV-Newspapers-285]

Several national and regional chains own and operate a number of local weekly newspapers, including the [Adams Publishing Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams_Publishing_Group),[^cite_note-287] [Sound Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_Publishing), [The Seattle Times Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seattle_Times_Company), and the [McClatchy Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McClatchy_Company).[^cite_note-BagwellStapilus-288] Free weekly newspapers include *[The Stranger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Stranger_\(newspaper\))*, *[Seattle Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Weekly)*, and the *[Inlander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inlander_\(newspaper\))*.[^cite_note-BagwellStapilus-288]<sup>: 18 </sup> The Seattle area also has a number of publications in English and other languages for ethnic communities, including the *[Seattle Chinese Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Chinese_Post)*, *[International Examiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Examiner)*, and *[Northwest Asian Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Asian_Weekly)*.[^cite_note-289] Since 2004, Washington has lost 37 local newspapers and seen the consolidation of smaller papers, including neighborhood and suburban papers in the Seattle metropolitan area.[^cite_note-LWV-Newspapers-285][^cite_note-290] Several newspapers have also switched to online-only publication, including Seattle's morning daily *[Post-Intelligencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Intelligencer)* in 2009.[^cite_note-291]

The state is divided into four [Designated Market Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_market) by [Nielsen Media Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_Media_Research): Seattle–Tacoma, which also extends east to Wenatchee; Portland, which includes most of Southwestern Washington; Spokane, which also includes northern Idaho; and Yakima–Pasco–Richland–Kennewick.[^cite_note-292] The Seattle–Tacoma market is the largest in the Pacific Northwest and has been the 13th largest in the United States since 2009.[^cite_note-293]

In 2009, Washington had 39 full-power television stations and an additional 11 from Portland, Oregon. Most are affiliated with a national or regional broadcasting network.[^cite_note-294] The state is home to 383 stations licensed with the [Federal Communications Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Communications_Commission) (FCC).[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] These radio stations broadcast to local markets and online, where Seattle-based music station [KEXP-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KEXP-FM) has found a worldwide following.[^cite_note-297]

## Health care

### Insurance

The top two health insurers as of 2017 were [Premera Blue Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premera_Blue_Cross), with 24 percent market share, followed by [Kaiser Permanente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser_Permanente) at 21 percent.[^cite_note-298] For the individual market, [Molina Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molina_Healthcare) had the top share at 23%.[^cite_note-299]

The state adopted the [Washington Healthplanfinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Healthplanfinder) system in 2014 after the passage of the federal [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act) (also known as "ObamaCare").[^cite_note-300] The system is used by approximately 90 percent of Washington residents who purchase or acquire their health insurance directly rather than through an employer.[^cite_note-301] The state's [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) program, named [Washington Apple Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Apple_Health), provides healthcare coverage to people with disabilities or low incomes.[^cite_note-302]

The state of Washington reformed its health care system in 1993 through the Washington Health Services Act. The legislation required individuals to obtain health insurance or face penalties, and required employers to provide insurance to employees. Health insurance companies were required to sell policies to all individuals, regardless of pre-existing conditions, and cover basic benefits.[^cite_note-303] The act was mostly repealed in 1995 before it could go into full effect.

### Facilities

See also: [List of hospitals in Washington (state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Washington_\(state\))

Hospitals exist across the state, but many of Washington's best-known medical facilities are located [in and around Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_facilities_of_Seattle). The Seattle–Tacoma area has six major hospitals: [Harborview Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harborview_Medical_Center), [University of Washington Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington_Medical_Center), [Seattle Children's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Children's), [Swedish Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Medical_Center), [MultiCare Tacoma General Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MultiCare_Tacoma_General_Hospital), and [St. Joseph Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Joseph_Medical_Center_\(Tacoma,_Washington\)).[^cite_note-304] The Seattle-area hospitals are concentrated on [First Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Hill), which is home to [Virginia Mason Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Mason_Medical_Center). The neighborhood has received the nickname "Pill Hill" owing to the high concentration of healthcare facilities.[^cite_note-305]

As of 2023, the state has over 14,000 total [hospital beds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_bed) that are licensed for [acute care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acute_care) in 93 facilities. Several religious healthcare providers, primarily [Catholic organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_health_care), control 49 percent of the state's hospital beds and have acquired and consolidated major systems in Washington.[^cite_note-306]

## Culture

### Sports

[Pickleball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickleball), a [racquet sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racquet_sport) invented on [Bainbridge Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bainbridge_Island) in 1965, was designated as Washington's official [state sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_sport) in 2022.[^cite_note-SR-Pickleball-307] For three years in a row, 2021, 2022 and 2023, the sport was named the fastest growing sport in the United States by the Sports and Fitness Industry Association (SFIA).[^cite_note-308]

#### Individual sports

The [Seattle Open Invitational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Open_Invitational) golf tournament was part of the [PGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Tour) from the 1930s to the 1960s. The [GTE Northwest Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GTE_Northwest_Classic) was part of the [Senior PGA Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_PGA_Tour) from 1986 to 1995, and the [Boeing Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_Classic) since 2005. In addition, the [2015 U.S. Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_U.S._Open_\(golf\)) was held at [Chambers Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chambers_Bay), and several major tournaments were held at [Sahalee Country Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahalee_Country_Club).

[Pacific Raceways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Raceways) is a motorsports venue that has hosted the Northwest Nationals of the [NHRA Mello Yello Drag Racing Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHRA_Mello_Yello_Drag_Racing_Series) and a round of the [Trans-Am Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Am_Series).

The [WTA Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTA_Seattle) tennis tournament was part of the [WTA Tour](1398173.md) from 1977 to 1982.

## Symbols, honors, and names

Four ships of the [United States Navy](20518076.md), including two battleships, have been named [USS *Washington*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Washington) in honor of the state. Previous ships had held that name in honor of George Washington.

### Unofficial state nickname

The state's nickname, "The Evergreen State",[^cite_note-Symbols\@WA.gov-1][^cite_note-309] was proposed in 1890 by Charles T. Conover of Seattle. The name proved popular as the forests were full of [evergreen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evergreen) trees and the abundance of rain keeps the shrubbery and grasses green throughout the year.[^cite_note-310] Although the nickname is widely used by the state, appearing on [vehicle license plates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_license_plate_designs_and_serial_formats) for instance, it has not been officially adopted.[^cite_note-Symbols\@WA.gov-1] A 2023 bill in the state legislature to formally recognize it as the [state nickname](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_nickname) was passed by the senate but was returned to committee.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312] [The Evergreen State College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Evergreen_State_College), a state-funded institution in Olympia, also takes its name from this nickname.

### State symbols

Main article: [List of Washington state symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Washington_state_symbols)

The [state song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_songs) is "[Washington, My Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,_My_Home)", the [state bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_birds) is the [Willow goldfinch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_goldfinch),[^cite_note-313] the state fruit is the apple, and the state vegetable is the [Walla Walla sweet onion](51258.md).[^cite_note-314] The state dance, adopted in 1979, is the [square dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_dance). The [state tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_tree) is the [western hemlock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_hemlock). The [state flower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_flower) is the [coast rhododendron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coast_rhododendron). The [state fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_fish) is the [steelhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_trout).[^cite_note-Symbols\@WA.gov-1] The [state folk song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_folk_song) is "[Roll On, Columbia, Roll On](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roll_On,_Columbia,_Roll_On)" by [Woody Guthrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woody_Guthrie). The unofficial, but popularly accepted, state rock song is "[Louie Louie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louie_Louie)".[^cite_note-315]

The state grass is [bluebunch wheatgrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluebunch_wheatgrass). The [state insect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_insect) is the [green darner dragonfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_darner). The [state gem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_gem) is [petrified wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrified_wood). The [state fossil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_fossil) is the [Columbian mammoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_mammoth). The [state marine mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_mammals) is the [orca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orca). The [state soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_soil) is [Tokul soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokul_soil).[^cite_note-316] The [state land mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_mammals) is the [Olympic marmot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_marmot).[^cite_note-Symbols\@WA.gov-1] The [state seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_Washington), featured in the state flag, was inspired by the unfinished portrait of President George Washington by [Gilbert Stuart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_Stuart).[^cite_note-317] The [state sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_sport) is [pickleball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickleball).[^cite_note-SR-Pickleball-307]
